## Task 10: Verify realtime poke is sent AFTER transaction commit

Approach: enumerated every poke emitter across the branch (`grep -rn "emitReplicachePokes\|replicacheEvents\.emit\|\.poke(\|pokeMembers" packages/api/src`) and traced each call against its surrounding transaction boundary. Cross-checked server-mutators.ts to confirm mutators never emit pokes from inside the savepoint. Read the whole poke bus contract (events/replicache-events.ts) to document the invariant for future contributors.

Call-site audit (one bullet per emitter, all verified post-commit):
  - packages/api/src/modules/replicache/push.ts:219 — `AssetMemberService.pokeMembers(assetId)` runs after `await db().transaction(...)` on line 98 resolves. The mutation loop collects `affectedAssetIds` into a Set inside the tx body and returns it; fan-out happens outside. Post-commit. ✅
  - packages/api/src/modules/replicache/push.ts:226 — `emitReplicachePokes([userId], '')` for user-scoped mutators (notificationMarkRead / notificationMarkAllRead). Same post-commit block. ✅
  - packages/api/src/modules/asset-members/service.ts:169 — `NotificationService.poke(existingUser.id, assetId)` in `AssetMemberService.invite` fires after the `await db().transaction(...)` on line 154-168 resolves. The transaction only contains the `assetMembers` insert + `NotificationService.record(tx, ...)` write — no poke inside. Post-commit. ✅
  - packages/api/src/modules/asset-members/service.ts:294 — `emitReplicachePokes([userIdToRemove], assetId)` in `AssetMemberService.removeMember` fires after the `await db().transaction(...)` on line 271-286 resolves. Same pattern as invite. Post-commit. ✅
  - packages/api/src/modules/asset-members/service.ts:82 — `pokeMembers` helper itself does a `SELECT + emit`; no transaction. Callers are responsible for ensuring they invoke `pokeMembers` after their own tx commits. ✅
  - packages/api/src/modules/moments/service.ts:67 — `pokeMembers(assetId)` after `await db().delete(...)` (no transaction). ✅
  - packages/api/src/modules/moments/service.ts:88 — `pokeMembers(assetId)` after `await db().insert(...).returning()` (no transaction). ✅
  - packages/api/src/modules/moments/service.ts:104 — `pokeMembers(updated.assetId)` after `await db().update(...).returning()` (no transaction). ✅
  - packages/api/src/modules/moments/service.ts:119 — same pattern as :104. ✅
  - packages/api/src/modules/comments/service.ts:47 — `pokeMembers(assetId)` after `await db().delete(...)` (no transaction). ✅
  - packages/api/src/modules/comments/service.ts:68 — `pokeMembers(assetId)` after `await db().insert(...).returning()` (no transaction). ✅
  - packages/api/src/modules/comments/service.ts:84 — `pokeMembers(updated.assetId)` after `await db().update(...).returning()` (no transaction). ✅
  - packages/api/src/modules/notifications/service.ts:77 — `emitReplicachePokes([recipientId], assetId)` inside `NotificationService.poke()`. The function itself has no tx; it's always called outside one. The JSDoc at service.ts:70-74 already documents "Call this AFTER the transaction that created the notification has committed". Both callers (asset-members:169 above) comply. ✅

Server-mutators.ts audit: grepped `emitReplicachePokes|pokeMembers|replicacheEvents` against `packages/api/src/modules/replicache/server-mutators.ts` — zero hits. Mutators only write to the DB via the savepoint `tx`; they never emit pokes themselves. The poke fan-out is centralized in push.ts post-commit, driven by the mutation loop's collected `affectedAssetIds` / `userPokeNeeded` summary. This is the right architecture: mutators describe effects, the orchestrator (push.ts) decides when to broadcast.

Issues found and fixed:
  1. packages/api/src/events/replicache-events.ts — Module had no top-of-file docblock stating the post-commit invariant. The invariant is load-bearing for correctness (a poke emitted inside an uncommitted tx causes stale pulls: the receiving client's pull runs on a different pool connection and sees the pre-commit snapshot, resulting in a silent miss until the next unrelated trigger). Added a module-level docblock explicitly naming every emitter entry point (`emitReplicachePokes`, `AssetMemberService.pokeMembers`, `NotificationService.poke`), stating the MUST-be-post-commit contract, explaining the failure mode it prevents, and pointing at push.ts:214-233 as the canonical fan-out pattern. Also enhanced the `emitReplicachePokes` JSDoc to restate the invariant at the call-site where a contributor would look for it.
  2. packages/api/src/events/replicache-events.ts:70-72 — Silent catch on `publisher.publish(...)` with no comment explaining why the fallback path exists. Per review-prompt.md Common Patterns #5 (silent error swallowing). Not a behavior change — the fallback still emits locally — but added a two-line comment identifying this as the "Redis unavailable" fallback path and calling out that other replicas will miss this specific poke (they'll catch up on the client's next regular pull interval). Matches the existing comment style in `events/asset-events.ts:95-97`.

Issues noted (no fix needed):
  1. packages/api/src/events/replicache-events.ts:70 — Floating Promise from `publisher.publish(...).catch(...)`. Intentional: the publish is fire-and-forget by design (the caller has already committed and returned; we don't want to block the HTTP response on Redis roundtrip). `.catch()` handles the only failure mode we care about (pub failure → local fallback). ioredis tracks the connection internally; any protocol-level errors surface through the shared ioredis error handler (set up in queue/connection.ts connection pool). Not a bug.
  2. packages/api/src/events/replicache-events.ts:62 — `.catch(() => {})` on unsubscribe during `closeReplicachePokeBridge`. Acceptable during teardown — at this point we're shutting down and any subscriber error is moot. Matches the pattern used in `closeRedis()` at queue/connection.ts:54.
  3. packages/api/src/modules/replicache/push.ts:218-220 — `for (const assetId of outcome.affectedAssetIds) { await pokeMembers(assetId); }` serializes the fan-out. Each `pokeMembers` does one SELECT + one publish loop; for a multi-asset push this adds latency to the HTTP response. Could be `Promise.all([...].map(pokeMembers))` to pipeline. Not a correctness issue; affectedAssetIds is typically size 1 in practice (a client pushes mutations for the asset it's currently viewing). Micro-optimization, not a Task 10 concern.
  4. packages/api/src/events/replicache-events.ts:75 — Local `replicacheEvents.emit('poke', event)` is synchronous. The SSE listener in modules/replicache/index.ts:81-84 writes into the stream controller synchronously. No race between publish and subscribe on single-replica deploys.
  5. packages/api/src/events/replicache-events.ts:39-46 — `subscriber.on('message', ...)` JSON.parse catch silently drops malformed messages. Acceptable — a malformed Redis message is either (a) another service publishing to our CHANNEL accidentally (misconfiguration, should be loud but not in this scope) or (b) a corrupted wire payload (recovery: next real poke). Could add a `console.warn` with truncated payload for debugging; low-signal, not worth adding now.
  6. packages/api/src/modules/asset-members/service.ts:82 — `pokeMembers` only fails if `listMemberUserIds` throws; the catch block warns and swallows (appropriate for a fire-and-forget notifier). The emitted pokes themselves don't throw (EventEmitter.emit and ioredis.publish are both fire-and-forget). Safe.
  7. push.ts user-scoped poke already runs inside a try/catch (push.ts:225-232). This is defensive over-engineering — `emitReplicachePokes` cannot realistically throw (it just pushes into an EventEmitter / Redis publisher). But the catch is cheap and future-proofs the call against publisher refactors. Kept as-is.
  8. The queue/connection.ts `connections` array captures both publisher and subscriber when `initReplicachePokeBridge()` runs. At shutdown `closeRedis()` calls `.quit()` on all of them. Lifecycle is correct. Verified.

Library improvements made:
  - None applicable. EventEmitter + ioredis pub/sub is the library primitive; no hand-rolled wrapper exists to collapse. The `publish() → emit()` fallback pattern is idiomatic ioredis usage for optional-Redis deploys (matches the existing pattern in events/asset-events.ts). The per-mutation savepoint in push.ts already uses Drizzle's nested `tx.transaction(...)` primitive (verified in Task 7) — there's no library that provides a built-in "poke on tx commit" hook; the manual post-commit fan-out IS the right library-shaped pattern.
  - Verified Drizzle has no `tx.onCommit()` hook via `rg -n 'onCommit|afterCommit' node_modules/.pnpm/drizzle-orm@*/node_modules/drizzle-orm/pg-core` — zero hits. Confirmed the "return summary from tx, fan out after await" pattern in push.ts is the canonical Drizzle-shaped approach. (Postgres has `LISTEN/NOTIFY` which emits on commit via `pg_notify`, but wiring that end-to-end is a larger architectural change and outside Task 10 scope; the current Redis pub/sub architecture is deliberate.)

Checklist verification (per review-prompt.md Security + Resilience + Code Quality + Common Patterns):
  - Every poke runs after `await tx.commit()` or outside the transaction block — verified by enumeration above. Zero in-tx poke emissions across the entire diff.
  - Server mutators never emit pokes from inside the savepoint — verified via grep; fan-out is centralized in push.ts post-commit.
  - Poke contract is documented at the bus entry point — new module-level docblock on replicache-events.ts names every emitter, the rule, the failure mode, and the canonical pattern location. Enhanced `emitReplicachePokes` JSDoc restates it at the call-site.
  - No sensitive data in logs — existing catch blocks use `err instanceof Error ? err.message : err` / `String(err)`. No new logging added that would leak.
  - No silent error swallowing unexplained — added "Redis unavailable" comment to the publish catch fallback so future readers see the intent. Subscriber-message catch kept silent (documented in issues-noted #5).
  - Proper error handling — every emitter call site has a try/catch or a fire-and-forget `.catch()`; push.ts:225-232 wraps even the user-scoped poke.
  - Idempotency — pokes are idempotent by nature (a poke says "pull"; pulling twice is cheap). Retry-safe.
  - Transactions for atomic operations — the poke IS the post-commit signal that the atomic unit succeeded. Emitting before commit would break atomicity from the client's perspective.
  - Reuse existing utilities — all poke fan-out goes through `emitReplicachePokes` or `AssetMemberService.pokeMembers` or `NotificationService.poke`. No ad-hoc `replicacheEvents.emit` call outside the bus module itself (bridge) and the SSE stream (subscriber).
  - pnpm check-types: clean (8/8 tasks green). @milkpod/api and downstream re-ran due to the replicache-events.ts doc edit (comment-only change; no type surface affected); @milkpod/{env,sync,ai,auth} cache-hit.

## Task 11: Scope realtime channels per user / per asset

Approach: traced the current pub/sub topology end-to-end (publisher → Redis channel → subscriber → local EventEmitter → SSE listener → HTTP response). Compared against the global-channel pattern in `events/asset-events.ts`. Verified ioredis@5.10.1's `SUBSCRIBE`/`UNSUBSCRIBE` semantics (built/redis/RedisOptions.js:45 confirms `autoResubscribe: true` — reconnect auto-re-subscribes, so our refcount state is the single source of truth and ioredis replays it after a transient drop). Chose per-user Redis channels (`replicache-pokes:u:<userId>`) over per-asset: the SSE connection is already one-per-user, and per-asset would force a subscription manifest that changes on every membership change — brittle. Per-user is the right grain for the way the SSE stream is authored, and the "client subscribes only to channels for assets it can see" invariant is enforced by the emission site (`emitReplicachePokes(userIds, assetId)` where `userIds` is already the membership-filtered recipient list — a user never receives a poke for an asset they can't see).

Prior state: one global Redis channel (`'replicache-pokes'`) carried every poke to every replica; the local EventEmitter re-emitted everything on a single `'poke'` event name; the SSE handler subscribed to ALL pokes and filtered by `event.userId !== userId` at the application layer. That matched review-prompt.md Common Patterns for "global broadcast channel" — every replica fanned in every user's pokes, then discarded ~N-1/N of them. Per the task's explicit "no global broadcast channel" rule, this needed a refactor, not a comment.

Issues found and fixed:
  1. packages/api/src/events/replicache-events.ts:46-71 (old) — Single global Redis channel `'replicache-pokes'` with server-side filtering at the SSE layer. Every replica received every poke and discarded the vast majority. Replaced with per-user channels `replicache-pokes:u:<userId>` and a dynamic subscribe/unsubscribe lifecycle driven by active SSE listeners. A replica only subscribes to channels for users it is currently serving, so cross-replica fan-in drops from O(total pokes) to O(pokes for my users). Reduces both bandwidth and the attack-surface for cross-user leakage (a replica that never serves user U never sees U's pokes in memory).
  2. packages/api/src/events/replicache-events.ts (old lines 81-86) — SSE handler performed the userId filter at the application layer: `if (event.userId !== userId) return;`. That filter was load-bearing for privacy on the single-global-channel architecture. Eliminated it by making the channel itself user-scoped and emitting on a per-user local event name (`poke:<userId>`). The SSE listener now only receives events addressed to its user — no application-level filter, no possibility of forgetting the check on future listener additions. Defense-in-depth: the subscriber callback also re-validates `event.userId === userId` before forwarding, guarding against a misaddressed publish.
  3. packages/api/src/modules/replicache/index.ts:81-96 — SSE handler's subscription was open-ended against a global event name, making per-user listener leaks invisible. Replaced with `subscribeUserPokes(userId, listener) → () => void`, which refcounts concurrent listeners for the same user and drives the Redis subscribe/unsubscribe pair automatically. Multiple tabs from the same user share ONE Redis subscription on this replica (the refcount guarantees idempotent subscribe/unsubscribe against ioredis).
  4. packages/api/src/events/replicache-events.ts:65 (old) — `replicacheEvents.setMaxListeners(200)` was a global cap on the bus regardless of how many connected users existed. Fine at 200 concurrent users; fragile as the app scales. Set to `0` (unlimited) since event names are now user-scoped — each user's `poke:<userId>` event has at most O(tabs-per-user) listeners, which is the natural bound.
  5. packages/api/src/events/replicache-events.ts (added `closeReplicachePokeBridge`) — Previously unsubscribed from ONE global channel at teardown. Now iterates the refcount map and unsubscribes from every active per-user channel, preventing stray Redis subscriptions from outliving the process's SSE listeners.

Issues noted (no fix needed):
  1. `packages/api/src/events/asset-events.ts` — uses the same global-channel pattern (`CHANNEL = 'asset-status'`) that Task 11 just fixed for replicache-events. Out of scope (Task 11 is scoped to replicache realtime channels), but the pattern is identical and a follow-up task could apply the same per-user scoping. Flagged here so it's discoverable. The asset-status stream is lower-volume than sync pokes (fires on transcription status changes, ~1-10 per asset per lifetime) so the scalability concern is smaller, but the privacy argument is equivalent.
  2. packages/api/src/events/replicache-events.ts subscribe failure path (line 146-153) — If the per-user `subscriber.subscribe(...)` rejects for any reason (transient Redis error, bad connection state), we log and swallow. The local `emitter.on(...)` listener is still installed, so same-replica pokes still reach the SSE stream via the `publish()` fallback path on this replica. Cross-replica pokes will be missed until the next resubscribe or reconnect (ioredis's `autoResubscribe: true` does not help here because WE own the SUBSCRIBE-per-user call, not an initial subscribe list). A future-hardening option: retry with exponential backoff, or promote to a user-facing error to trigger client-side fallback to polling. Low priority — the SSE connection will be torn down and re-established if the stream itself errors, which re-runs the subscribe.
  3. `subscriber.on('message', ...)` → `JSON.parse` catch silently drops malformed payloads. Acceptable per Task 10's audit (issues-noted #5); a malformed Redis message is a misconfiguration or corruption, not a normal runtime condition.
  4. Per-user channel names embed the userId verbatim. UserIds in this project are `nanoid()` strings (alphanumeric), so they are Redis-channel-name-safe (no wildcards, no control bytes, no whitespace). Verified by reading `packages/auth/src/schema.ts` — the `user.id` column uses `createId()` from nanoid. If the userId format ever changes to include `*` or `?` (Redis pattern glob chars), `subscribe()` still treats it as a literal (only `psubscribe()` treats as glob), so our usage is safe in perpetuity.
  5. Client-side SSE connection is still one-per-user (not one-per-asset). Multiple open asset views from the same user share one SSE stream and the client pulls on any poke for any of its assets. This matches how Replicache's pull model works (full-CVR diff; pulling for asset B is cheap if B is already up-to-date). Per-asset client channels would require separate EventSource connections per asset, which is wasteful. Per-asset filtering on the receiving client is possible via the `assetId` field on the poke payload but not currently needed — the client's `rep.pull()` is idempotent and the CVR protocol skips no-op patches.

Library improvements made:
  - `ioredis@5.10.1` exposes variadic `subscribe(...channels)` / `unsubscribe(...channels)` and maintains its own subscription set (`built/SubscriptionSet.d.ts` in the package). Leveraged this to batch unsubscribe at teardown (`subscriber.unsubscribe(...channels)`) rather than looping — one RESP round-trip instead of N.
  - Used ioredis's `autoResubscribe: true` default (verified in `built/redis/RedisOptions.js:45`) — on transient connection drops, ioredis replays all active subscriptions. This means our refcount map IS the canonical view of "what this replica is subscribed to", and we don't need a manual resubscribe loop after reconnect. Documented this assumption in the issues-noted #2.
  - No hand-rolled wrapper emerged to collapse. The `publish() → emitter.emit()` fallback for the no-Redis case is idiomatic ioredis usage (same shape as events/asset-events.ts); there is no library primitive that spans "pub/sub with optional single-replica mode" — you either have Redis or you don't.
  - Considered but rejected: Redis `PSUBSCRIBE` with pattern `replicache-pokes:u:*`. It would centralize one pattern subscription per replica, simpler bookkeeping, but re-introduces the "every replica sees every poke" fan-in property we just eliminated. Rejected on scalability grounds.

Checklist verification (per review-prompt.md Security + Resilience + Code Quality + Common Patterns + Library Leverage):
  - Channel names include the userID — every publish goes to `replicache-pokes:u:<userId>` (packages/api/src/events/replicache-events.ts:52); no global channel exists anywhere in the module.
  - Client subscribes only to channels for assets it can see — the SSE connection is user-scoped (`/api/replicache/events` requires auth, extracts `user.id`, subscribes ONLY to `poke:<userId>`). The emission side (`emitReplicachePokes(userIds, assetId)`) is called with membership-filtered `userIds` by every caller (traced in Task 10's audit), so the user only receives pokes for assets they have access to. Defense-in-depth: the Redis subscriber callback re-validates `event.userId === userId` against the channel name before forwarding.
  - No sensitive data in logs — new `console.warn` on subscribe failure uses `err instanceof Error ? err.message : err` (packages/api/src/events/replicache-events.ts:149-152). No poke payloads, user emails, or connection info logged.
  - No silent error swallowing unexplained — `unsubscribe().catch(() => {})` at teardown is documented as best-effort cleanup (matches the pattern audited in Task 10 issues-noted #2). The new subscribe-failure catch produces a specific `console.warn`; not silent.
  - No `any` — `PokeListener = (payload: ReplicachePoke) => void` is fully typed; `subscribeUserPokes` returns `() => void`; `userIdFromChannel` returns `string | null`.
  - No unjustified `!` — zero in the diff.
  - Proper HTTP status / error handling — unchanged at the HTTP layer; the SSE stream's error path is preserved by Elysia.
  - Idempotency — `subscribeUserPokes` is refcounted; two tabs for the same user ⇒ one Redis subscribe, one unsubscribe when both close. `emitReplicachePokes` remains idempotent (a duplicate poke just triggers a no-op pull).
  - Reuse existing utilities — `subscribeUserPokes` is now THE helper for SSE listeners; the SSE handler consumes it directly (no ad-hoc `emitter.on('poke', ...)` anywhere). Also removes the need for the old exported `replicacheEvents` singleton from the module's public surface (only internally held as `emitter`).
  - Library leverage — leveraged ioredis variadic unsubscribe + autoResubscribe (documented above). Used Node's built-in `EventEmitter` per-event-name routing so per-user listeners don't share a listener array, eliminating the need for an application-layer filter.
  - Transactions for atomic operations — unchanged; Task 10 already verified all poke emitters fire post-commit.
  - Common Patterns #5 silent error swallowing — every catch in the module either logs a specific warning or is documented inline as best-effort cleanup.
  - pnpm check-types: clean (8/8 tasks green). @milkpod/api and @milkpod/server/web re-ran (cache miss from signature changes on replicache-events.ts: new `subscribeUserPokes` export, removed `replicacheEvents` from public exports). @milkpod/{db,sync,env,ai,auth} cache-hit.

## Task 12: Add Elysia auth guard and input validation to Replicache routes

Approach: verified Elysia 1.4.22's `.guard()` + macro interaction by reading `node_modules/.pnpm/elysia@1.4.22/.../dist/index.d.ts:1319-1392` (the two `guard` overloads) and confirming via mcp__grep__searchGitHub against `elysiajs/elysia` that `.guard({ auth: true }, (app) => ...)` with a named macro propagates the resolved `{ user }` context into child handlers (test/types/lifecycle/soundness.ts:488, test/types/macro.ts:9, elysiajs/elysia-lucia example/index.ts:47). The two-arg form (`guard(hook, run)`) is the canonical pattern for scoping auth to a subset of routes on the same instance — matches this exact case (pull/push/events all want auth; nothing else on this Elysia instance needs a different policy). Also verified `maxItems` is a first-class TypeBox ArrayOption in `@sinclair/typebox@0.34.41/build/esm/type/array/array.d.mts:9`, and that Elysia's default `ValidationError.status = 422` (dist/error.js:128) — so a TypeBox cap yields 422 by default, not 413. Task explicitly asks for 413 on oversized push, so the logical cap is enforced by an explicit handler check with a higher TypeBox `maxItems` as defense-in-depth.

Prior state:
  - Three routes (`POST /pull`, `POST /push`, `GET /events`) each carried their own `{ auth: true }` option. Duplication matches the codebase-wide convention (packages/api/src/modules/*/index.ts all use per-route auth), but this task is scoped to applying the `.guard()` refactor specifically to replicache routes per review-prompt.md library-leverage checklist.
  - Body validators existed inline in index.ts: `clientGroupID` was `t.String({ minLength: 1, maxLength: 200 })`, `cookie` was `t.Nullable(t.Number())`, mutations array had no `maxItems`, and sub-fields (`id`, `timestamp`, `clientID`, `name`) had zero constraints.
  - `PullRequestBody` / `PushRequestBody` / `PushMutationV1` were hand-written interfaces in pull.ts / push.ts that duplicated the TypeBox schema structure — two sources of truth with no mechanism to catch drift. Handlers accepted `body as PullRequestBody` / `body as PushRequestBody` casts because Elysia's inferred body type (from the inline schema) was not the same nominal type as the hand-written interface.
  - No oversized-push guard; a pathological client could POST a 100k-mutation batch and the handler would try to process each one.

Issues found and fixed:
  1. packages/api/src/modules/replicache/index.ts (entire file) — Three per-route `{ auth: true }` options replaced with a single `.guard({ auth: true }, (app) => app.post(...).post(...).get(...))` block. Fewer places to forget auth, and the guard is the canonical Elysia primitive for scoping lifecycle hooks to a subroute group (verified via docs/elysia.md + GitHub examples). The `authMacro`'s `resolve` still runs per-request, so auth behavior is unchanged; only the declaration site is deduplicated.
  2. packages/api/src/modules/replicache/index.ts (push route) — Added `if (body.mutations.length > ReplicacheModel.MAX_MUTATIONS) return status(413, { message: ... })` before calling `handlePush`. Semantically correct HTTP code for "logical limit exceeded" (RFC 9110 §15.5.14 — Payload Too Large). Previously no cap existed; a client could push any array length up to Elysia's default body-size limit.
  3. packages/api/src/modules/replicache/model.ts (new file, 52 lines) — Created following the existing `AssetMemberModel` namespace pattern (packages/api/src/modules/asset-members/model.ts). Extracts body schemas into `ReplicacheModel.pull`, `ReplicacheModel.push`, `ReplicacheModel.pushMutation`; uses `typeof X.static` to derive the TS types (`Pull`, `Push`, `PushMutation`). Declared the two constants `MAX_MUTATIONS = 100` (logical cap, handler-enforced, 413) and `HARD_MUTATION_LIMIT = 1000` (memory safety cap, TypeBox-enforced via `maxItems`, 422). Defense in depth: even if the handler forgets to check, the TypeBox layer rejects anything >1000.
  4. packages/api/src/modules/replicache/pull.ts:27 and push.ts:20-21 — Replaced hand-written `PullRequestBody` / `PushRequestBody` / `PushMutationV1` interfaces with `type PullRequestBody = ReplicacheModel.Pull` (and siblings) so the runtime validator and the handler signature share ONE source of truth. Adding a field to the TypeBox schema now automatically updates the handler's body type; forgetting to update the validator produces a TS error at the handler. Previously drift was silent.
  5. packages/api/src/modules/replicache/index.ts (pull + push handlers) — Removed `body as PullRequestBody` / `body as PushRequestBody` casts. Unnecessary after step 4: Elysia's inferred body type from the schema IS `ReplicacheModel.Pull` (same nominal type as the handler parameter). Cleanest: no `as` cast anywhere in the router. Verified via `pnpm check-types` — no type errors.
  6. packages/api/src/modules/replicache/model.ts — Tightened sub-field validators beyond the prior skeleton: `cookie` went from `t.Nullable(t.Number())` to `t.Nullable(t.Integer({ minimum: 0 }))` (server writes cookies as integer `cvrVersion` counters, never floats or negatives); `profileID` went from bare `t.Optional(t.String())` to `maxLength: 200`; `schemaVersion` got `maxLength: 50`; each mutation's `id` and `timestamp` became `t.Integer({ minimum: 0 })`; `clientID` got `minLength: 1, maxLength: 200`; `name` got `minLength: 1, maxLength: 100`. Rejects malformed or hostile inputs at the validator, well before they touch the transaction or the mutator dispatch.

Issues noted (no fix needed):
  1. Other module routers (`asset-members/index.ts`, `moments/index.ts`, `comments/index.ts`, etc.) still use per-route `{ auth: true }` rather than `.guard()`. Consistent with the codebase-wide convention; Task 12 is scoped to replicache routes only. A sweep-style task could migrate all modules to `.guard()` but that's out of scope here and not a correctness concern (per-route `{ auth: true }` is functionally equivalent — just less concise).
  2. `mutation.args: t.Unknown()` at the Elysia validator layer. Intentional. The push handler dispatches `parsed = mutatorArgsSchemas[name].safeParse(args)` with the per-mutator zod schema from `@milkpod/sync` BEFORE running the mutator (verified in push.ts:140-149). Elysia-layer validation of `args` would require duplicating the sync package's zod schemas in TypeBox, which introduces the same drift risk Task 12 step 4 just eliminated. Zod-at-dispatch is the right layer for args validation.
  3. `pushVersion: t.Literal(1)` / `pullVersion: t.Literal(1)` — Hard-coded. Replicache's protocol is currently at pull v1 / push v1; if Replicache bumps to v2, we'll need a migration plan anyway (the entire CVR schema may change). Accepting a literal is the right constraint for today.
  4. The 413 check is in the handler rather than as a standalone `beforeHandle` hook. Could be extracted if the cap ever needs reuse, but a one-line inline check is clearer at the call site.

Library improvements made:
  - Replaced inline per-route `{ auth: true, body: ... }` with `.guard({ auth: true }, (app) => app.post(...).post(...).get(...))` — canonical Elysia primitive for subgroup lifecycle. Reference: docs/elysia.md + elysiajs/elysia test/types/lifecycle/soundness.ts:488.
  - Used TypeBox's native `ArrayOptions.maxItems` (`@sinclair/typebox@0.34.41/.../array.d.mts:9`) for the HARD_MUTATION_LIMIT — Elysia wires it into the pre-handler validator automatically; no hand-rolled length check needed at the TypeBox layer. Saves one line vs. a `beforeHandle` alternative.
  - Used TypeBox's native `IntegerOptions.minimum` and `StringOptions.minLength/maxLength` for sub-field constraints. No hand-rolled validation in the handler (would duplicate what TypeBox already does).
  - Derived `PullRequestBody = typeof pull.static` (and siblings) via Elysia's built-in `.static` helper on TypeBox schemas — no hand-maintained TS interfaces. Reference: the existing `AssetMemberModel.Invite = typeof invite.static` pattern in packages/api/src/modules/asset-members/model.ts:8.
  - Considered but rejected: putting the 413 check in TypeBox via `maxItems: 100`. Elysia's `ValidationError.status = 422` (verified in `dist/error.js:128`) — doesn't give the semantically-correct 413 that the task prescribes, and a custom `.onError()` mapping `ValidationError` to 413 would only fire for mutation-count failures (messy). Handler-level check with a higher TypeBox cap is the right split.

Checklist verification (per review-prompt.md Security + Resilience + Code Quality + Library Leverage):
  - Input validation on every route — pull and push both have TypeBox schemas covering every field (type, constraints). `/events` is GET with no body.
  - Auth + authorization checks on every endpoint — all three routes sit inside `.guard({ auth: true }, ...)`; the `authMacro.resolve` returns 401 if session missing. Per-asset authz is downstream (pull.ts's `getAccessibleAssetIds` + push.ts's mutator ACLs, audited in Tasks 6 + 8).
  - Proper HTTP status codes — 401 (auth macro), 403 (forbidden clientGroup binding), 413 (oversized push), 422 (TypeBox validation failures). No 500 for client mistakes.
  - No magic numbers — `MAX_MUTATIONS = 100` and `HARD_MUTATION_LIMIT = 1000` extracted to `ReplicacheModel` constants with comments explaining their respective roles. Referenced by name in the handler and in the schema.
  - Reuse existing utilities — followed the `AssetMemberModel` namespace convention for schema/type colocation; reused the `authMacro` via guard. No new auth or validation helper introduced.
  - Proper error handling with meaningful messages — 413 response includes the cap in the message for client debuggability (`Push exceeds 100 mutations`). No sensitive information (no SQL, no stack trace, no connection state).
  - No `any` — zero `any` anywhere in the diff. Even the `body` parameter is inferred from the TypeBox schema.
  - No unjustified non-null assertions — zero `!` in the diff.
  - Discriminated unions + exhaustive switches — `handlePull` / `handlePush` return `{ forbidden: true }` as a discriminant checked via `'forbidden' in result`. Handler's two branches cover the union exhaustively (result is either forbidden or the success response).
  - Library leverage — `.guard()` + TypeBox native options (documented above). No hand-rolled wrapper.
  - pnpm check-types: clean (8/8 tasks green). @milkpod/api re-ran (cache miss from the new model.ts and router structural changes); @milkpod/server re-ran downstream; @milkpod/web cache-miss too. @milkpod/{env,sync,db,auth,ai} cache-hit — schema/model changes were module-local.

## Task 13: Register Replicache routes in the rate limiter

Approach: read `packages/api/src/middleware/rate-limit.ts` top-to-bottom to understand the token-bucket + `categorize()` + identity-keying flow, confirmed `packages/api/src/index.ts:131` mounts `rateLimiter` before `.use(replicache)` at line 180 (so the plugin's `onBeforeHandle` does fire for `/api/replicache/*`). Reviewed the `session-cache` layer (`getSessionCached`) to confirm the user-keyed path: `category !== 'auth'` → look up session → key as `user:<id>:<category>`. Replicache routes are behind `authMacro` + `.guard({ auth: true })` (Task 12), so the session will always exist when the limiter runs — the `ip:` fallback only fires if Better Auth is transiently broken. Sampled the existing bucket sizes (`LIMITS`) to pick a "generous" number: crud is 100/min burst + 100/min refill. Replicache is a hot sync path that fires pull on connect, on every Redis poke, and on every push ack — multi-user collab can easily hit 5–10 pull/s per user. Sized at 600 burst + 10/s sustain (600/min) — ~6x crud — so normal collab traffic never trips and sustained >10 req/s per user (the anomaly we actually want to limit) does.

Prior state: `/api/replicache/pull`, `/api/replicache/push`, and `/api/replicache/events` did NOT appear in `categorize()` → fell through to `return null` → limiter returned early → **no rate limiting at all** on the sync hot path. A hostile or buggy client could spam pulls/pushes as fast as the DB would service them. Matches review-prompt.md Common Patterns #3 ("New endpoints not covered by rate limiter").

Issues found and fixed:
  1. packages/api/src/middleware/rate-limit.ts:66-74 — `RateCategory` union had no `'replicache'` member → no dedicated bucket existed for sync traffic. Extended to `'ingest' | 'chat' | 'crud' | 'auth' | 'billing' | 'replicache'`. The `satisfies Record<RateCategory, BucketConfig>` on `LIMITS` now forces a compile error if a new category is added to the union without sizing — type-driven completeness (review-prompt.md TypeScript patterns: "satisfies for config objects").
  2. packages/api/src/middleware/rate-limit.ts:80-83 (new) — Added a dedicated `replicache: { capacity: 600, refillRate: 600 / 60 }` bucket. Separate from chat/ingest/crud per the task spec. Inline comment documents the sizing rationale ("Sync hot path: pull/push fire on every user action and every realtime poke") so future reviewers understand why it's 6x larger than crud. Generous but bounded — a sustained ≥10 req/s per user is anomalous and the cap kicks in.
  3. packages/api/src/middleware/rate-limit.ts:91 — Added `if (path.startsWith('/api/replicache')) return 'replicache';` to `categorize()`. Single prefix covers all three sub-routes (`/pull`, `/push`, `/events`) — including the SSE connection-establish on `/events`, which also deserves a bucket so a reconnect storm can't DOS the limiter.
  4. User-keyed (not IP) — verified by tracing the existing limiter flow: line 137 starts with `identity = ip:<ip>`, then line 138 `if (category !== 'auth')` triggers a `getSessionCached()` lookup and overwrites to `user:<id>` when available. Since replicache is NOT `auth` and is always authenticated (Task 12's `.guard({ auth: true })` rejects unauth requests before the route runs), the limiter will always key on `user:<id>:replicache` for legitimate traffic. No code change needed on this axis — the existing control flow already does the right thing for the new category.

Issues noted (no fix needed):
  1. Position of `/api/replicache` in `categorize()` — placed AFTER `/api/auth/` and `/api/billing` (both literal prefixes that cannot collide with `/api/replicache`), and BEFORE the generic crud fall-through block. No ordering concern: `/api/replicache/*` shares no prefix with any other category. The `/api/replicache` prefix has no trailing slash so it matches `/api/replicache`, `/api/replicache/pull`, `/api/replicache/push`, and `/api/replicache/events`; we never mount anything outside the `/api/replicache/*` tree, so a bare `/api/replicache` GET is a 404 from Elysia regardless.
  2. `/events` SSE is a long-lived GET — it consumes ONE token at connect and holds the connection for hours. The limiter's `onBeforeHandle` does NOT fire for each SSE message (messages go over the same HTTP connection, not new requests), so the bucket only gates connect/reconnect, which is the right behavior. A reconnect storm still hits the 600-burst ceiling, preventing amplification; normal steady-state is free. No separate `replicache-sse` bucket needed.
  3. Bucket TTL (line 22: 10 minutes idle) — same as other categories. A user who stops using the app for 10 minutes resets to full capacity on next pull. Correct behavior — the limiter's purpose is anti-abuse, not resource accounting.
  4. Per-category `consume()` is called serially in a single request (one category per path). Replicache pulls never share a bucket with chat/crud/ingest — the `:replicache` suffix on the bucket key isolates the hot path. A user hammering pulls cannot starve their chat or crud quota, which is exactly why the task spec required "separate from chat/ingest/crud".
  5. `getSessionCached()` is called for each replicache request. It already has a two-layer cache (per-Request WeakMap + 10-s token cache), and since the authMacro also calls `getSessionCached()` downstream, the limiter's call is free for any request that has already been authed in the same per-request path. The WeakMap dedupes; only the first lookup hits the DB. Zero extra latency on the sync hot path from rate-limiting.

Library improvements made:
  - Used `satisfies Record<RateCategory, BucketConfig>` (already in place, preserved) — extending `RateCategory` AND forgetting to size `LIMITS.replicache` now produces a TS error at the `satisfies` call site. Type-driven completeness instead of a runtime lookup miss. No hand-rolled union-key check needed.
  - No new library primitive in play — Elysia's `onBeforeHandle` + Node's `setInterval` for the bucket sweep is already the canonical pattern for token-bucket rate limiting, and the codebase's existing token bucket is correctly written (capacity-clamped refill, monotonic `lastRefill`, purge-on-idle). Nothing to replace. Reviewed whether a library like `elysia-rate-limit` would help — it does `ip`-only keying by default with an opt-in `generator()` for custom keys (verified by reading `node_modules/.pnpm/elysia-rate-limit@*/...` is NOT present; searched github.com/rayriffy/elysia-rate-limit README). The existing hand-rolled limiter is ~130 lines and already handles user-vs-IP keying, per-category configs, and in-process state without an extra dep — replacing it would regress expressiveness (no per-category + per-identity compound keying off-the-shelf). Deferred.
  - Considered but rejected: moving the per-route rate-limit config onto the replicache module (e.g. via a `.beforeHandle()` on the router). Rejected because a single global `categorize()` function is the existing convention and is easier to audit ("all rate-limited paths live here"). Per-route decoration would scatter the policy across 20+ files and make it easy to forget a new route.

Checklist verification (per review-prompt.md Security + Resilience + Code Quality + Common Patterns + Library Leverage):
  - Rate limiting on sensitive endpoints — `/api/replicache/*` now covered. Verified `categorize()` returns `'replicache'` for all three sub-routes via prefix match. Sizing (capacity: 600, refillRate: 10/s) is generous but bounded.
  - New endpoints covered by the rate limiter's `categorize` function — yes, per Common Patterns #3's explicit language ("Check that the path prefix appears in the categorize function"). `/api/replicache` is now one of the if-else branches.
  - User-keyed (not IP) — verified via the existing control flow: non-auth categories attempt a session lookup and overwrite `identity` from `ip:<ip>` to `user:<id>` when a session is available. Replicache is always authenticated (guard Task 12), so keys are always `user:<id>:replicache`.
  - Dedicated bucket separate from chat/ingest/crud — `replicache` is a new enum member in `RateCategory`; its `LIMITS.replicache` config is distinct; the bucket key suffix isolates the hot path from all other categories.
  - No magic numbers — `capacity: 600` and `refillRate: 600 / 60` are written with an inline comment explaining the rationale (~6x crud; 10 req/s sustain is the anomaly threshold). The other LIMITS entries use the same numeric literal style, so this matches convention.
  - No sensitive data in logs — unchanged; the limiter logs nothing. 429 response message is a constant ("Too many requests. Please try again later."), no user/session/path data.
  - Proper HTTP status codes — 429 with `Retry-After` header preserved. Existing pattern.
  - Proper error handling — `getSessionCached()` failures fall through to IP keying (line 144 catch) — unchanged and correct for replicache (a missing session triggers authMacro's 401 downstream, which sits behind the limiter). The IP fallback just means an unauth client is rate-limited by IP on the replicache bucket, which is strictly more lenient than blocking on the session-lookup error — safe default.
  - No `any` — zero `any` in the diff. `RateCategory` is a string-literal union; `LIMITS` is typed via `satisfies`.
  - No unjustified non-null assertions — zero `!` in the diff.
  - Exhaustive typing — `LIMITS` uses `satisfies Record<RateCategory, BucketConfig>`; adding a category without sizing is a TS error. `categorize()` returns `RateCategory | null`, and callers check for `null` before indexing `LIMITS`.
  - Library leverage — already-idiomatic token-bucket + Elysia hook; no wrapping or reinvention. Considered and rejected `elysia-rate-limit` (less expressive for our compound keying).
  - Common Patterns #3 ("New endpoints not covered by rate limiter") — resolved for replicache. Note: other recently-added modules (`/api/notifications`, `/api/asset-members`) are NOT in the current `categorize()` either, but those are outside this task's scope (review-plan.md Task 13 is explicitly scoped to replicache routes). Flagging here for any future sweep task.
  - pnpm check-types: clean (8/8 tasks green). @milkpod/api re-ran (cache miss from the `RateCategory` union extension and `LIMITS` literal change); @milkpod/server + @milkpod/web re-ran downstream. @milkpod/{env,db,sync,ai,auth} cache-hit — rate-limit.ts is internal to packages/api and doesn't cross a package boundary.

## Task 14: Prepare the hot-path pull query

Approach: before touching code, verified Drizzle 0.44.7's `.prepare(name)` semantics by reading `node_modules/.pnpm/drizzle-orm@0.44.7_*/node_modules/drizzle-orm/pg-core/query-builders/select.js:760-788` (the `_prepare(name)` method binds to the session captured on the query builder), `pg-core/session.d.ts:16-43` (PgPreparedQuery wraps query+params+cache; execute(placeholderValues) calls `fillPlaceholders(params, values)` then the underlying client), and `node-postgres/session.js:165-178` + `:180-196` (transaction creates a new NodePgSession bound to a dedicated PoolClient; prepareQuery stores `this.client` on the NodePgPreparedQuery). Cross-checked via mcp__grep__searchGitHub on drizzle-team/drizzle-orm — every official prepared-statement example builds at module scope on the pool-level db; there are ZERO examples of prepared statements inside `.transaction()` callbacks. Confirmed the task's literal prescription ("module-scope, not recreated per call") is in direct tension with running the query inside the pull's tx: module-scope prepared statements bind to the pool and check out their own connection, bypassing any surrounding tx on the caller side. Resolved by picking the one query that legitimately doesn't need tx semantics — the notifications row-version lookup (pure-read, user-scoped, no dependency on earlier tx state, self-healing on the next pull if a race produces a slightly-stale snapshot).

Prior state: `packages/api/src/modules/replicache/pull.ts:197-218` ran an inline `tx.select(...).leftJoin(...).where(eq(notifications.recipientId, userId)).orderBy(asc(notifications.id))` on every pull. No name, no placeholder — node-postgres sent it as an unnamed extended-protocol query, which forces PostgreSQL to parse + plan it per execution (the server-side plan cache is keyed on statement NAME, never on unnamed statements). Additionally, Drizzle regenerated the SQL string from the query-builder IR on every pull (a JS-side cost), even though the shape never changed. Hot-path: pulls fire on every user action + every realtime poke, so this query ran many times per minute per active user.

Issues found and fixed:
  1. packages/api/src/modules/replicache/pull.ts:197-218 (old) — Inline tx-bound `.select().from(notifications).leftJoin(user).where(eq(recipientId, userId)).orderBy(asc(id))` with no prepared-statement name. Every pull paid: (a) Drizzle query-builder SQL generation, (b) node-postgres unnamed-statement parse + plan on the backend. Extracted to a module-scope prepared statement `replicache_pull_notifications` with `sql.placeholder('userId')` for the recipient filter. Now: SQL generated ONCE at first call for the process lifetime; PostgreSQL prepares the statement once per PoolClient (first use on that connection) and reuses the cached plan on every subsequent pull from that client. Hot-path amortizes to pure `EXECUTE <name>($1)`.
  2. packages/api/src/modules/replicache/pull.ts:22-76 (new) — Added `_notificationsPullStmt` singleton + `buildNotificationsPullStmt()` + `getNotificationsPullStmt()` lazy-init trio. Lazy init mirrors the existing `getCVRStore()` pattern in cvr.ts so construction happens on first call (after DB pool is live + DATABASE_URL is set), not at module import. Using `ReturnType<typeof buildNotificationsPullStmt>` for the singleton's type keeps the TS type tracking Drizzle's generic prepared-query type without hand-writing `PgPreparedQuery<…>`.
  3. packages/api/src/modules/replicache/pull.ts (the execute call) — Replaced the 22-line inline query with `await getNotificationsPullStmt().execute({ userId })`. The `execute()` signature requires the placeholder-keyed record, so `.execute({ userId })` is enforced at the type level — forgetting a placeholder produces a TS error.
  4. packages/api/src/modules/replicache/pull.ts (the inline comment at lines 250-258) — Documented that the notifications read runs on the pool (not the tx) and why this is safe under READ COMMITTED. Without this, a future reader could reasonably assume the read shares the tx's snapshot and be surprised.

Issues noted (no fix needed):
  1. `getAccessibleAssetIds(userId, tx)` at pull.ts:166 — Also a per-user simple lookup (single `userId` parameter, fixed shape, hot-path). A module-scope prepared statement would plausibly save per-pull cost here too. NOT done because (a) the existing inline comment at pull.ts:158-165 explicitly documents the design choice to run this query on the tx's connection alongside the entity reads for narrowing the concurrent-revocation window, and (b) the task's wording "per-user row-version lookup" specifically describes the notifications query (which returns rowVersion as the primary signal for diff detection); membership is an asset-id lookup, not a row-version lookup. Preserving the existing tx-bound design avoids a correctness regression in exchange for a marginal JS-overhead improvement. Can be revisited under a follow-up task if profiling shows it matters.
  2. `assetMoments` + `assetComments` reads at pull.ts:170-192 — Both filter by `inArray(assetId, assetIds)` where `assetIds` is a runtime array whose length varies per user. Drizzle + PG can prepare this with a single-array placeholder (verified: `inArray(col, sql.placeholder('ids'))` → backend `WHERE col IN ($1)` where $1 is the full array) but the resulting prepared plan uses PG's generic plan heuristics which are suboptimal for array-length-dependent choices (nested-loop vs hash vs index scan). Left unprepared so PG can replan per call. Also, these queries MUST run in the tx because they depend on `assetIds` computed from the tx's membership read; moving them to the pool would open a race where membership was read on tx but entities filtered on pool.
  3. `replicacheClientGroup` lookup (pull.ts:119-122) and `replicacheClient` list (pull.ts:333-340) — Both MUST stay in the tx: the clientGroup lookup is gated by the advisory lock (pull.ts:115) and the subsequent INSERT/UPDATE; the client list must read LMIDs consistent with the cvr_version bump. Preparing either would require running them on a pool connection where the advisory lock is NOT held, breaking the tx's invariants.
  4. The `user` join in the authors-metadata read (pull.ts:272-284) — Dynamic `inArray(userTable.id, authorIds)`. Same concern as #2; left unprepared.
  5. Prepared-statement name collision across deploys — node-postgres prepares statements PER CONNECTION on the PG backend. The `name` ("replicache_pull_notifications") is client-scoped (the statement is torn down when the connection closes) so rolling deploys + new pool connections on the new code will re-prepare with the same name transparently. No need for a `_v1` suffix or manual DEALLOCATE. Verified by reading node-postgres's `Connection.prototype.parse` usage.
  6. Module-scope singleton is process-local; there is no cross-process coordination. Not needed — PG's prepared-statement cache is per-connection, and each Node process has its own pool, so the singleton correctly matches the process-local invariant.

Library improvements made:
  - Used Drizzle's native `.prepare(name)` + `sql.placeholder('userId')` rather than a hand-rolled SQL template + manual parameter binding. This is the idiomatic library primitive for "cache the query plan + reuse the SQL string"; no wrapper code needed. Reference: `node_modules/.pnpm/drizzle-orm@0.44.7_*/node_modules/drizzle-orm/pg-core/query-builders/select.js:763-788` (the `_prepare` + `prepare` pair on PgSelectBase).
  - Used `ReturnType<typeof buildNotificationsPullStmt>` to derive the singleton's TS type instead of hand-writing the full `PgPreparedQuery<PreparedQueryConfig & { execute: … }>` signature. Pattern documented in `docs/typescript-patterns.md` (derived types from function signatures).
  - Lazy-init singleton via a mutable let + getter function. Matches the existing `getCVRStore()` pattern (cvr.ts:78-84) — one pattern for "process-local lazy singleton" across the replicache module, discoverable by grep. No new pattern introduced.
  - Considered but rejected: preparing on the tx handle inside the transaction callback (`tx.select(...)...prepare('name').execute(...)`). The PG backend plan cache would still work (same connection, same name = cached plan), but the Drizzle SQL-builder IR would rebuild per-call. That's the same cost we're trying to eliminate — the task text's "module-scope, not recreated per call" is unambiguous about avoiding this.
  - Considered but rejected: preparing a raw `sql<...>` template at module scope and executing with `tx.execute()`. Drizzle's `tx.execute(sql)` does not support placeholder filling (`fillPlaceholders` is only called from inside `PgPreparedQuery.execute`) — we'd have to hand-build the parameter array per call. Loses the type safety benefits of Drizzle's query builder.
  - Considered but rejected: preparing `getAccessibleAssetIds` at module scope too. Would produce a second prepared statement with the same single-`userId` shape, but conflicts with the documented design choice at pull.ts:158-165 to run it on the tx. Would require either removing that comment + design (regression) or accepting both paths (inconsistent, harder to reason about). Left as-is; documented in issues-noted #1.

Checklist verification (per review-prompt.md Library Leverage + Backend Queries & Data Access + Code Quality):
  - Library leverage (Drizzle: `.prepare('name')` with `sql.placeholder`) — exactly the library primitive the task asks for. No hand-rolled SQL string or manual parameter binding.
  - No N+1 queries — unchanged; notifications is a single joined read for all of the user's notifications, and the module-scope prepare doesn't change the query shape.
  - Appropriate database indexes — the query's `WHERE recipientId = $1 ORDER BY id` is covered by `notification_recipient_created_idx` (recipientId + createdAt) for the filter; `ORDER BY id` is ascending-on-primary-key so PG uses the primary-key index for ordering. `notification_recipient_row_version_idx` (recipientId, rowVersion) exists too and covers rowVersion-filtered reads if we ever add one. Confirmed in packages/db/src/schema/notifications.ts:67-79.
  - Pagination — notifications query is intentionally unpaginated here because the CVR diff protocol requires seeing ALL visible notifications to compute the del set. The total-per-user volume is bounded by user activity; a future task (review-plan.md Task 21) will paginate the REST `list()` endpoint for the UI, but the sync pull's unbounded scan is correct-by-design.
  - Transactions for atomic operations — unchanged. The CVR version bump + clientGroup lookup still run inside `db().transaction(...)` (pull.ts:113-368). The notifications read now runs on a pool connection, but that's pure-read and the tx doesn't need to include it for correctness (documented inline).
  - Use parameterized queries — `sql.placeholder('userId')` produces a PG-protocol parameter ($1), not a string interpolation. Zero injection surface; type-checked at execute time.
  - Appropriate data types — prepared statement inherits the column types from the Drizzle schema (notifications.rowVersion is integer; createdAt is timestamp returning Date via Drizzle's column mapper). No type changes.
  - No magic numbers or hardcoded strings — the prepared-statement name `'replicache_pull_notifications'` is a string literal, but it's appropriate as a literal: (a) it's used once, (b) PG treats it as a literal identifier not a config value, (c) there's nothing else to derive it from. Matches how every Drizzle .prepare() call in the wild writes a literal name.
  - Proper error handling — execute() propagates errors the same way the old inline query did (promise rejection → propagates out of handlePull → returned as a 500 by the Elysia handler). No new error paths introduced.
  - No `any` — zero. The prepared statement's execute return type is inferred by Drizzle as `Array<{ id: string; recipientId: string; type: NotificationType; ... actorName: string | null; actorImage: string | null }>`, same shape that `NotificationService.serialize` expects.
  - No unjustified non-null assertions — zero `!` added.
  - Exhaustive switch / discriminated unions — unchanged; `NotificationService.serialize` already has an exhaustive switch on `NotificationType` (packages/api/src/modules/notifications/service.ts:128-148).
  - SSR / browser guards — N/A (server-only module).
  - Common Patterns — no new fetch() or console.error added; no opacity-0 hover class; no silent catch; no magic number; no missed switch.
  - pnpm check-types: clean (8/8 tasks green). @milkpod/api re-ran (cache miss from pull.ts structural changes); @milkpod/server + @milkpod/web re-ran downstream (cache miss). @milkpod/{env,db,sync,ai,auth} cache-hit.

## Task 15: Audit asset-members/service.ts for membership-scoped queries

Approach: audited every method of AssetMemberService + its Elysia router. Primary thrust per task spec: "Role capability enforcement at the service layer: viewer attempting write → 403. Grep each mutation method for role check." Found that all three mutation methods (`invite`, `removeMember`, `revokeInvite`) were trusting the router's preflight `isOwner()` check without any service-layer enforcement — a single-level protection that would break the moment a non-router caller appeared (future sync mutator, admin CLI, migration script). Verified with `rg 'AssetMemberService\.'` that service is only called from router + moments/comments/push (pokeMembers only). Safe to tighten signatures.

Library verification before touching code:
  - Read `node_modules/.pnpm/drizzle-orm@0.44.7_*/node_modules/drizzle-orm/pg-core/query-builders/insert.d.ts:138-141` for `onConflictDoNothing({ target?, where? })` signature. Confirmed: target accepts `IndexColumn | IndexColumn[]`; omitting defaults to the table's PK. For assetMembers (PK on (assetId, userId)) no target needed; for assetInvites (unique constraint on (assetId, email)) target must be specified.
  - Checked `packages/db/src/schema/asset-members.ts:54` — `role: assetMemberRoleEnum('role').$type<'editor' | 'viewer'>().notNull()` confirms the TS type for invites.role is already narrowed, making the runtime `.filter((i): i is PendingInviteRow => ...)` in list() tautological.
  - Verified via `packages/db/src/migrations/0034_asset_invite_role_not_owner.sql` review (Task 3) that the CHECK constraint also enforces this at the DB level — so the filter is dead runtime code AND a redundant type predicate.
  - Confirmed only the replicache module currently uses `.guard({ auth: true }, app => ...)` (`rg '\.guard\('` → 1 hit). Per the library leverage checklist, repeated `{ auth: true }` per route on the 4 asset-members endpoints should be collapsed into a single guard block.
  - No Drizzle relations defined in `packages/db/src/schema/` (`rg 'relations\(' packages/db/src/schema` → 0 hits), so the relational query API (`db.query.table.findFirst({ with: {...} })`) is not available without adding a relations file. Left the manual innerJoin in list() as-is — one join, well-scoped; adding a relations schema just to eliminate 1 explicit join is a larger refactor outside Task 15's scope.

Issues found and fixed:
  1. packages/api/src/modules/asset-members/service.ts:136-228 — `invite()` took `invitedBy` and trusted the caller had already owner-gated it. No service-layer role check. Added `const callerRole = await AssetMemberService.getRole(assetId, invitedBy); if (callerRole !== 'owner') return { kind: 'forbidden' };` at the top. Extended InviteResult with `{ kind: 'forbidden' }`. Router updated to 403 on this branch (was also 403 before, but now via service contract, not a preflight check).
  2. packages/api/src/modules/asset-members/service.ts:274-319 — `removeMember()` same story. Added `callerRole !== 'owner' → { removed: false, reason: 'forbidden' }` at the top of the method, BEFORE looking up the target's role (so a non-owner can't probe membership by trial-and-error — the 403 response leaks nothing). Extended `RemoveMemberResult` type to include `'forbidden'` reason. Replaced the loose `{ removed: boolean; reason?: ... }` with a discriminated union `{ removed: true } | { removed: false; reason: ... }` so TS enforces reason presence on the failure branch.
  3. packages/api/src/modules/asset-members/service.ts:321-338 — `revokeInvite()` didn't take a caller userId at all. Added `revokedBy` parameter; added owner check; changed return type from `Promise<boolean>` to `RevokeInviteResult = { revoked: true } | { revoked: false; reason: 'not_found' | 'forbidden' }`. Router now passes `user.id` as the third argument.
  4. packages/api/src/modules/asset-members/service.ts:150-194 — `invite()` existing-user path did `SELECT role; if null INSERT` — classic check-then-insert TOCTOU. Two concurrent invites for the same email would both see null and both attempt insert; the second throws on PK violation → bubbles as a 500 instead of the correct `already_member` response. Replaced with a single `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING RETURNING` inside the transaction. If `returning()` is empty, the row existed already → return `already_member`. If non-empty, we have the inserted row and can proceed with the notification record + pokes. Eliminated 1 DB roundtrip (the pre-SELECT) and closed the race atomically at the PG level.
  5. packages/api/src/modules/asset-members/service.ts:196-228 — Same check-then-insert pattern on the invite path. Replaced with `.insert(assetInvites).values({...}).onConflictDoNothing({ target: [assetInvites.assetId, assetInvites.email] }).returning({ id: assetInvites.id })`. Must specify target explicitly here — the unique is on a composite (assetId, email), not the PK. Empty returning → `already_invited`. Eliminated the pre-SELECT.
  6. packages/api/src/modules/asset-members/service.ts:95, 269, 315 — Three catch blocks used the pattern `err instanceof Error ? err.message : err`. Per Common Patterns #1 in review-prompt.md: "console.error('ctx', err) logs the FULL error object — connection strings, query state, stack traces. Always use `err instanceof Error ? err.message : String(err)`." Changed all three to `: String(err)`. Thrown values that are not Error instances can carry arbitrary structure — `String(err)` coerces to a safe scalar.
  7. packages/api/src/modules/asset-members/service.ts:121-122 — `members as MemberRow[]` cast. Removed — Drizzle's .select({...}) inference already matches MemberRow exactly. Verified column by column: `userId`, `role` (typed as `'owner' | 'editor' | 'viewer'` from the enum = `AssetMemberRole`), `invitedBy: string | null`, `createdAt: Date` (notNull from lifecycle_dates), and the joined `name: string`, `email: string`, `image: string | null`. No cast needed.
  8. packages/api/src/modules/asset-members/service.ts:123-126 — `invites.filter((i): i is PendingInviteRow => i.role === 'editor' || i.role === 'viewer')`. The schema's `$type<'editor' | 'viewer'>()` already narrows the compile-time type of `invites[n].role`; the DB-level CHECK constraint (migration 0034) guarantees the runtime values match. The filter was dead code at both levels. Removed the filter and the type predicate; renamed the local from `invites` to `pendingInvites` to match the return shape. Added an inline comment pointing at the $type + CHECK pairing so the next reader doesn't re-add the filter defensively.
  9. packages/api/src/modules/asset-members/index.ts (entire file) — Router was using per-route `{ auth: true }` on all four endpoints, plus explicit `isOwner()` preflights on POST/DELETE endpoints. Wrapped all routes in `.guard({ auth: true }, (app) => app...)` (matches the replicache module's pattern from Task 12). Dropped the per-route preflights because the service now enforces owner. Net: saves 1 DB roundtrip per POST/DELETE request (the preflight), simpler code, and role-check logic lives in exactly one place (the service). Response shapes unchanged for the success paths (`{ success: true }` for both DELETEs; narrowed InviteResult for POST member path), so frontend's Eden treaty consumers at `apps/web/src/components/share/collaborators-section.tsx` are uneffected.
 10. packages/api/src/modules/asset-members/index.ts — Router did `if (result.kind === 'already_member') ... if (result.kind === 'already_invited') ... return result;` — not exhaustive. Adding a new InviteResult discriminant would silently fall through. Replaced with `switch (result.kind)` having explicit cases + `default: { const _exhaustive: never = result; throw new Error(...); }`. Same for `removeMember`'s and `revokeInvite`'s reason discriminant on the failure branch. Compile-time coverage now — extending RemoveMemberResult/InviteResult without handling new cases breaks the build.

Issues noted (no fix needed):
  1. `AssetMemberService.list()` returns all members + all pending invites for an asset with no limit. Per review-prompt.md pagination rule. Left unpaginated because (a) the member count is bounded by asset owners' willingness to invite, in practice <20, (b) the share dialog UI renders all rows at once, (c) adding pagination now would require a cursor shape change + frontend rework. Flagged for a follow-up if any asset approaches hundreds of members. Task 15 scope is service/router correctness, not UI scale.
  2. `AssetMemberService.getRole()` is called TWICE in `removeMember()` (once for caller, once for target). Could be fused into a single query that returns both rows via `inArray(userId, [removedBy, userIdToRemove])`, but the extra complexity isn't justified — the query uses the (assetId, userId) PK so each lookup is O(1) with a planner-cached plan and executes in sub-ms. Left as two explicit calls for readability.
  3. `AssetMemberService.list(assetId)` doesn't take a userId. It trusts the router to have gated via `getRole(params.id, user.id)` first. Considered adding a caller userId parameter to make the service self-authorizing, but the pattern would be redundant: the 404 branch currently returned by the router IS the authz enforcement (it doesn't distinguish "no such asset" from "not a member"). Moving it into service.list() would require the same caller check — no improvement, just shifted. Left as-is.
  4. `/api/assets/*` is already covered by the rate limiter's `crud` bucket (rate-limit.ts:96-104), so no categorize() change needed — confirmed by reading the current file.
  5. `sendInviteEmail` still has no outer timeout. Review-prompt says every external API call needs `AbortSignal.timeout(ms)`. Deliberately NOT touching this in Task 15 — Task 17 is specifically scoped to the invite email flow ("Add timeout and generic failure path to invite email send"). Leaving it in the queue to avoid scope creep.
  6. The `notifications/service.ts` file has `// eslint-disable-next-line @typescript-eslint/no-explicit-any` + `type DbTx = any`. Not a Task 15 file (notifications module is its own task in Phase 4), but noting as a cross-task observation. Proper fix is `type DbTx = ReturnType<typeof db> | Parameters<Parameters<ReturnType<typeof db>['transaction']>[0]>[0];` (the pattern used in replicache/authz.ts:38-40). Out of scope here.
  7. `AssetMemberService.list()` runs its members join read and its pendingInvites read as two awaited queries. Could `Promise.all` to parallelise. Micro-optimisation — the share dialog is not a hot path and both queries hit small tables. Left serial for readability.

Library improvements made:
  - Replaced 2× `SELECT ... INSERT ... 500-on-duplicate` patterns with Drizzle's native `.onConflictDoNothing()` (one with default PK target, one with explicit composite-unique target). This is the review-prompt.md's explicit library-leverage nudge: "Are there INSERT ... check for existing ... INSERT patterns that should be `db.insert().onConflictDoNothing()`?" The new code uses RETURNING to distinguish inserted vs conflicted atomically, giving the same semantics as the old check-then-insert WITHOUT the TOCTOU window. Reference: drizzle-orm@0.44.7/pg-core/query-builders/insert.d.ts:126-141.
  - Replaced per-route `{ auth: true }` on 4 endpoints with a single `.guard({ auth: true }, (app) => app...)` block, matching the replicache module's pattern from Task 12. Library-leverage nudge: "Are there repeated `{ auth: true }` options on every route that should use `.guard({ auth: true }, app => ...)`?" Answer: yes — done. Net: 3 fewer `{ auth: true }` literals, single auth boundary at the router definition.
  - Replaced the wide `{ removed: boolean; reason?: ... }` union with a proper discriminated union `{ removed: true } | { removed: false; reason: ... }`. Same treatment for revokeInvite's result. TS now enforces `reason` exists on the failure branch — callers writing `if (!result.removed) { ... result.reason ... }` get full narrowing without null/undefined checks. Pattern: docs/typescript-patterns.md § discriminated unions.
  - Changed `InviteResult` from a 4-member union to a 5-member union with a `'forbidden'` variant. The router's `switch` + never-default enforces exhaustiveness — extending the service's auth surface (e.g. adding `'asset_deleted'` for a soft-delete world later) would force a compile error at the router, not a silent fallthrough.
  - Removed the tautological `.filter((i): i is PendingInviteRow => ...)` in `list()`. The schema's `$type<>()` + CHECK constraint pair already guarantee the narrowing at both levels, so the filter was dead code. Library-leverage: trust the type system AND the DB constraint rather than re-validating in application code.
  - Removed the `as MemberRow[]` cast. Drizzle's select inference matches the interface exactly once the schema's types are respected. Library-leverage: prefer inference over casts (per review-prompt.md TypeScript checklist).

Checklist verification (per review-prompt.md Backend + Library Leverage + Common Patterns):
  - No N+1 queries — `invite()` now runs at most 3 queries in the member path (role, user, insert+notification tx), 3 in the invite path (role, user, insert). `removeMember()` runs 3 (caller role, target role, delete+notification tx). `revokeInvite()` runs 2 (role, delete). No loops containing queries; no unbounded fanout.
  - Appropriate database indexes — `getRole()` uses the (assetId, userId) PK of asset_member. `invite()` uses (email) index on user table (unique, from auth schema) and the (assetId, userId) PK / (assetId, email) unique on asset_member/asset_invite. `removeMember()` uses the same PKs. All hot reads indexed.
  - Pagination on list endpoints — `list()` unpaginated; noted above as acceptable for v1 (bounded by invites per asset).
  - Transactions for atomic operations — `invite()` existingUser path runs INSERT membership + INSERT notification in a single db.transaction(). `removeMember()` runs DELETE membership + INSERT notification in a single tx. Both patterns match the pre-task code, extended with onConflictDoNothing in the invite path.
  - Use parameterized queries or ORM methods — all Drizzle query-builder calls; no raw sql. `onConflictDoNothing({ target: [...] })` uses column references, not string literals.
  - Appropriate data types in schemas — unchanged.
  - Input validation on every route — body validated via `AssetMemberModel.invite` (email with format + maxLength 320, role as literal union). Path params come via Elysia's router typed as strings — service doesn't trust them (uses ORM eq() parameterization). No raw SQL.
  - Auth and authorization checks on every endpoint — Elysia `.guard({ auth: true })` at the router; service layer enforces owner role on every mutation (new in this task).
  - Rate limiting on sensitive endpoints — /api/assets/* covered by the crud bucket in categorize() at rate-limit.ts:96.
  - No sensitive data in logs — three catch blocks now use `String(err)` instead of bare `err`. No console.error in this module; only `console.warn` on best-effort side effects (poke failures, email lookup failures). Neither logs query state.
  - No secrets in error responses — all status() calls use generic messages ("Only the owner can...", "Member not found", "User is already a member"). No upstream provider errors bubble.
  - Proper HTTP status codes — 400 owner_protected (business rule / bad request), 403 forbidden, 404 not_found, 409 already_member/already_invited. Matches the spec in review-prompt.md.
  - Webhook endpoints — N/A (no webhooks touched).
  - Timeouts on external API calls — `sendInviteEmail` is still sans-timeout. Explicitly left for Task 17; noted above.
  - Idempotency — both INSERT paths are now `onConflictDoNothing`. `removeMember` re-check: concurrent deletes for the same (assetId, userId) both succeed at the DB level (the second affects 0 rows, would still end up with `{ removed: true }` from our side because we gate on the pre-delete role lookup). Edge case: two concurrent removeMember calls for the same user — both pass the role check (both see the same role), both call DELETE. One of them affects the row, the other affects 0. Both return `{ removed: true }`. Both call `NotificationService.record` — two identical notification rows get inserted (no unique constraint on notifications by resourceId+recipientId+type). Minor duplicate-notification issue, not Task 15's scope (notification idempotency is a Task 20/21 concern).
  - Connection pooling — unchanged.
  - Environment variables — unchanged. `serverEnv().CORS_ORIGIN` is the only env read.
  - Reuse existing utilities — `getRole()` is the shared membership lookup; all three mutation methods use it. No duplication.
  - Exhaustive switch statements — router now has three `default: { const _exhaustive: never = ... }` branches (POST /members, DELETE /members/:userId, DELETE /invites/:inviteId). Service's InviteResult / RemoveMemberResult / RevokeInviteResult discriminants are forced through the switch.
  - No magic numbers or hardcoded strings — role literals are typed via AssetMemberRole / AssetInviteRole unions; error messages are static strings; no hidden magic.
  - Proper error handling — swallowed errors in catch blocks use warn+message. Service returns discriminated failure kinds rather than throwing for expected-fail paths (forbidden, not_found, already_X). Unexpected errors (DB failure, etc.) propagate as 500 via Elysia's default.
  - Migrations safe — no schema changes in this task.
  - Common Patterns #1 — resolved (3 sites swapped from `err` → `String(err)`).
  - Common Patterns #2 — sendInviteEmail still untimeouted; deferred to Task 17.
  - Common Patterns #3 — /api/assets/* already categorized; no change needed.
  - Common Patterns #6 — service-layer authz extraction reduces a duplicated pattern (the isOwner preflight was copy-pasted in 3 places in the router).
  - Common Patterns #7 — exhaustive switch added in three router handlers.
  - No `any` — zero `any` introduced. All new types are proper discriminated unions.
  - No unjustified `!` — zero `!` introduced.
  - `as` casts — removed the `as MemberRow[]` cast; zero casts in the new code.
  - SSR / frontend — N/A (service + Elysia router, server-only).
  - pnpm check-types: clean. All 8 tasks green. @milkpod/api re-ran (cache miss from service.ts + index.ts diffs); @milkpod/server + @milkpod/web re-ran downstream. @milkpod/{env,db,sync,ai,auth} cache-hit.
